The information-theoretic acquisition functions are designed to maximize information gain, which could help the model better understand the function's behavior.
Let's consider the specific characteristics of the information-theoretic acquisition functions:
- qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum
- qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value
- qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value
- qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum
Given the poorly calibrated model (extremely large lengthscales), qPES might be the best choice because it focuses on the location of the optimum, which could help the model better understand the function's behavior in the search space.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.022), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.024), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.025), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.026), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.024), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.024), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.025), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.025), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.
Let's consider the information-theoretic acquisition functions. Among them, qPES was successful at N=10, but failed at N=12 and N=19. qMES, qJES, and qKG have all failed in the past.
Given that the function has not improved (f_min = -0.619), we're likely in a promising region. Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.026), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.024), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.021), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

Let's consider the specific characteristics of the acquisition functions:
1. PI (Probability of Improvement): Focuses on the probability of improving over the current best. It's simple but can be too conservative.
2. LogPI (Log Probability of Improvement): Similar to PI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
3. EI (Expected Improvement): Considers both the probability of improvement and the magnitude of improvement. It's a standard choice for exploitation.
4. LogEI (Log Expected Improvement): Similar to EI but uses the log to avoid numerical issues.
5. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound): Balances exploration and exploitation by adding a confidence bound to the mean prediction.
6. PosMean (Posterior Mean): Focuses on the mean prediction, which is purely exploitative.
7. PosSTD (Posterior Standard Deviation): Focuses on areas of high uncertainty, which is purely exploratory.
8. TS (Thompson Sampling): Samples from the posterior distribution, which can balance exploration and exploitation.
9. qKG (Knowledge Gradient): Maximizes the expected improvement in the knowledge of the optimum.
10. qPES (Predictive Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the location of the optimum.
11. qMES (Max-value Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the maximum value.
12. qJES (Joint Entropy Search): Maximizes the expected information gain about the joint distribution of the optimum and its value.
Given the very small lengthscales (mean 0.021), the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an acquisition function that can handle high sensitivity. The traditional exploitation-focused acquisition functions (PI, LogPI, EI, LogEI, PosMean) have all failed in the past, so we should avoid them.

EI: Given only 1 iteration remains, we need to focus on exploitation in the promising region we've identified. The function has not improved (f_min = -0.619) for several iterations, indicating we're in a promising region but not making progress. The very small lengthscales (mean 0.021) suggest the function is extremely sensitive, so we need an exploitation-focused function that can handle high sensitivity. EI (Expected Improvement) is a standard exploitation-focused function that has been used successfully in the past (N=35) and is robust to small changes in the input space.
Rosenbrock: The optimization process has been focused on a function with a very narrow, deep minimum at f_min = -0.619, extremely small lengthscales (mean 0.021), and a very short distance (0.01495) from the last point to the nearest other point. These characteristics match the classic Rosenbrock function (which has a narrow valley leading to its minimum at (1,1) with f(1,1)=0) when shifted downward by 0.619 to achieve the observed minimum value of -0.619. The function's extreme sensitivity around the minimum (very small lengthscales) is a hallmark of the Rosenbrock function, which is known for being challenging to optimize due to its narrow, curved valley.
